Entropy optimization of magnetohydrodynamic Casson hybrid nanofluid flow over a rotating disk with Cattaneo Christov heat flux
Authors: Tigabu Gubena, Wubshet Ibrahim
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
This study investigates unsteady rotating-disk transport of a water-based Casson nanofluid (Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O) and a Casson hybrid nanofluid (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O in a porous medium under magnetic forcing, suction, heat source/sink, and thermal radiation, while accounting for thermal relaxation to model non-Fourier heat conduction. Using similarity transformations, the governing multi-physics equations are reduced to a coupled nonlinear system for the radial and tangential velocities and temperature. To obtain reproducible semi-analytical benchmark solutions, the Homotopy Analysis Method (HAM) is employed with convergence-control parameters selected via \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}-curves and residual-error minimization. A central contribution is a controlled comparison between Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O and (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O under identical parameter sets, focusing on velocity and temperature characteristics. The results show that Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O yields higher velocity levels, whereas the hybrid nanofluid sustains higher temperatures within the thermal boundary layer, revealing a thermal-hydraulic trade-off for aqueous rotating systems. The magnetic parameter M physically quantifies Lorentz-force damping, leading to reduced velocities and modified thermal fields, while the thermal relaxation parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _t$$\end{document} delays the heat-flux response and tends to suppress temperature diffusion, thereby lowering the temperature profile. For the hybrid nanofluid, the skin-friction and Nusselt-number trends obtained from the plots are in good agreement with the tabulated values, confirming the consistency of the solution. Physically, increasing M enhances the skin-friction coefficient because stronger Lorentz braking demands greater wall shear to maintain the flow, while increasing Nr elevates the Nusselt number by strengthening radiative heat transport and steepening the wall temperature gradient. In addition, the thermodynamic irreversibility of the system is characterized through entropy generation and the Bejan number. Physically, increasing Br intensifies entropy generation because stronger viscous dissipation converts more mechanical energy into irreversible thermal energy, whereas increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _t$$\end{document} increases the Bejan number by making thermal irreversibility more dominant relative to frictional irreversibility under stronger non-Fourier heat-flux effects. The findings are relevant to rotating-disk cooling, porous heat-exchanger components, and nano-assisted aqueous lubrication/thermal management in compact and micro-scale rotating devices.

Introduction

Many industrial and bioengineering fluids depart from Newton’s linear stress–strain-rate law and exhibit yield-stress and shear-thinning/thickening responses. Such non-Newtonian behavior strongly alters near-wall momentum transport, boundary-layer development, and the associated heat/mass transfer rates, particularly in high-shear configurations (e.g., rotating machinery, disk reactors, porous rotating components, and coating/spin processes). Recent reviews and surveys emphasize that incorporating realistic rheology is essential for credible prediction of drag, wall shear, and thermal performance in modern thermal-management and process systems [1–3].

Among yield-stress models, the Casson fluid is widely adopted to capture an effective yield-stress-like behavior and shear-thinning character, which may arise from microstructural networks, particle chaining, or concentrated suspensions. Contemporary studies continue to employ Casson rheology as a practical constitutive choice for non-Newtonian heat-transfer media and nanoparticle-laden fluids under electromagnetic forcing and surface-driven shear [4, 5]. In rotating-disk settings, the strong azimuthal shear, cross-flow coupling, and sensitivity to suction/porosity/radiation make Casson-type modeling particularly relevant, because yield-stress effects can meaningfully modify the thickness of momentum and thermal layers and thus change both frictional and heat-transfer metrics [5, 6].

Nanofluids-engineered suspensions of nanoparticles in base liquids are used to enhance thermal transport by increasing effective thermal conductivity and, under some conditions, convective heat-transfer coefficients. However, a recurring practical challenge is stability: aggregation, sedimentation, and clogging can compromise both long-term performance and hydraulic reliability. A recent critical review on water-based hybrid nanofluids highlights that the benefit of thermal enhancement is tightly coupled to dispersion quality, sonication/surfactant protocols, pH control, and optimized volume fraction, and it stresses that stability constraints must be treated as a first-order design consideration (not an afterthought) [7]. A broader regulatory/technology-focused review likewise underscores that “performance gains” must be evaluated jointly with viscosity penalties, pumping power, and operational feasibility [1, 8].

Hybrid nanofluids (HNFs), which combine two nanoparticle types, are often pursued to obtain synergistic thermo-physical improvements that are difficult to achieve with a single nanoparticle family. Recent syntheses and application-facing reviews report that hybridization can improve thermal conductivity and tailor specific heat, but also warn that hybrid systems may intensify viscosity growth and stability problems if particle selection and mixing ratio are not optimized [2, 3, 7]. The addition of hybrid nanoparticles significantly enhances thermal transport, and this effect becomes stronger in the presence of thermal radiation, porosity, and Cattaneo–Christov thermal relaxation, which further modify the energy transport process [9]. The interfacial nanolayer also supports this enhancement by improving the thermophysical behavior of the suspension [10]. These effects are reflected in skin friction, the Nusselt number, and entropy generation, while porosity and the Brinkman number generally increase irreversibility and the Brinkman number tends to reduce the heat transfer rate [11].

The present investigation adopts a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{Cu}+\mathrm {Fe_3O_4})$$\end{document}/water hybrid Casson nanofluid. This selection is motivated by both thermal-performance and practical considerations. Copper nanoparticles provide very high intrinsic thermal conductivity and are widely used as benchmark metallic additives for enhancing conductive heat transport in nanofluid formulations [2, 8, 12]. In contrast, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Fe_3O_4}$$\end{document} (magnetite) nanoparticles are magnetically responsive and form the basis of many ferrofluid concepts, enabling strong coupling between external magnetic fields, microstructure alignment/chaining, and effective thermo-physical transport [13–15].

Their combination offers a synergistic effect: Cu primarily strengthens conductive heat transport, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Fe_3O_4}$$\end{document} strengthens the MHD response and magneto-thermal controllability. This synergy is particularly relevant in the present magnetohydrodynamic rotating-disk configuration, where Lorentz forces and magnetic interactions can significantly reshape near-wall shear and thermal gradients. Importantly, recent experimental evidence directly supports the relevance of Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}–Cu/water hybrid systems under magnetic fields. For example,[16] experimentally examined Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}/water and Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}–Cu/water hybrid nanofluids in a compact plate heat exchanger under an applied magnetic field, demonstrating that magnetic-field action can materially change effectiveness and convective performance and must be carefully balanced against viscosity-driven hydraulic penalties. Such results reinforce the need for coupled (momentum–thermal–MHD) modeling that can quantify trade-offs between heat-transfer enhancement and flow resistance under magnetic control.

Water is selected as the primary base fluid (with the option of water–ethylene glycol mixtures where application constraints require altered freezing/boiling points or viscosity control) because it is abundant, non-toxic, low-cost, and widely used in practical cooling and heat-exchange systems [1, 8]. A water-based \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{Cu}+\mathrm {Fe_3O_4})$$\end{document} hybrid nanofluid is therefore directly relevant to rotating-disk cooling, porous heat-exchanger components, and nano-lubricated rotating machinery where aqueous coolants remain standard. Where EG-based coolants are needed, recent work continues to analyze Cu nanoparticle performance in ethylene glycol for heat-transfer applications, supporting the feasibility of water-EG blending in practical designs [17]. Copper (Cu) nanoparticles are mainly applied in heat-transfer enhancement, thermal management, and energy-conversion systems [18, 19], whereas Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document} nanoparticles are commonly applied in magnetic nanofluids, cooling technologies, biomedical uses, waste eater treatment,removal of organic dyes and inorganic pollutantsfrom water, and environmental processes [20–22].

At the same time, choosing metal/metal-oxide hybrid nanoparticles avoids some of the cost, processing complexity, and health/environmental uncertainty that can be associated with carbon-nanotube-based hybrids. Recent toxicology-focused reviews and systematic evidence syntheses emphasize that CNT health impacts depend strongly on morphology, surface chemistry, and exposure route, and they continue to motivate caution for large-scale deployment and occupational exposure scenarios [23, 24]. Hence, Cu–Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document} hybrids provide a practically attractive alternative pathway to strong thermo-magnetic functionality without relying on CNT additives.

Magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) provides an additional control lever: the applied magnetic field induces Lorentz forces that can damp or redirect motion and alter the thermal boundary layer, while magnetic nanoparticles (e.g., Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}) introduce magneto-thermal responses that can further modify effective conductivity and convective transport [13–15]. Experiments on magnetic-field-dependent thermal conductivity in water-based magnetic hybrid nanofluids (e.g., Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}/CNT–water and Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}/graphene–water) show that thermal conductivity can increase with magnetic-field strength and orientation, highlighting the broader principle that magnetic action can be used to tune transport properties, albeit with system-dependent outcomes [13].

In addition, classical Fourier heat conduction can overpredict thermal propagation speed in micro-/meso-scale systems or high-rate processes. Consequently, non-Fourier heat-flux models (including the Cattaneo–Christov (CC) framework) are increasingly coupled with nanofluid transport models to capture thermal relaxation effects. Recent rotating-disk studies explicitly adopt CC heat flux together with nonlinear thermal radiation and hybrid nanofluids, demonstrating that relaxation parameters can reduce temperature fields and reshape wall heat-transfer rates [4, 6]. Such findings support incorporating thermal relaxation in rotating-disk nanofluid modeling when aiming for physically credible thermal predictions in rapidly evolving or strongly driven boundary layers.

From a second-law perspective, entropy generation analysis is now standard for quantifying thermodynamic irreversibility due to heat transfer, viscous dissipation, and (in MHD) electromagnetic effects. Recent contributions in hybrid-nanofluid and CC contexts continue to show that irreversibility metrics are sensitive to radiation, magnetic forcing, and relaxation parameters, and thus provide an optimization-relevant complement to first-law measures like Nusselt number and skin friction [25, 26]. The Bejan number, in particular, offers a compact indicator of whether heat-transfer irreversibility or frictional irreversibility dominates, enabling interpretable design trade-offs for thermal-management devices.

Rotating systems are configurations such as rotating disks and rotor–stator cavities in which fluid flow and heat transfer are altered by the motion of the rotating surface and the resulting centrifugal and Coriolis effects; recent work has examined these systems using combined numerical simulation and analysis [27]. These systems are important in thermal technology because they appear in applications such as turbine-disk cooling, rotating machinery, and related thermal-management devices where efficient removal of heat is essential for performance and reliability [27, 28].

In the more specific case of rotating-disk and disk-like boundary-layer flows, suction and surface stretching provide direct mechanisms to control the near-wall momentum and thermal layers, particularly for Casson-type (yield-stress) suspensions whose apparent viscosity depends strongly on shear. Recent numerical studies show that rotation changes the flow structure and thermal field inside disk cavities, thereby directly affecting local and average heat-transfer rates [27, 29]. Wall suction extracts low-momentum/high-thermal-energy fluid from the boundary layer, typically thinning the velocity and thermal layers and modifying diffusion-dominated transport, whereas stretching adds a surface-imposed strain rate that intensifies near-wall shear and can strengthen advection relative to diffusion. Recent Casson studies on stretching–rotating disks and stretched porous surfaces confirm that these controls can significantly reshape both velocity and temperature fields under MHD action and internal heating/cooling effects [30, 31]. Experimental work on rotor-stator systems has further confirmed that rotating-surface modifications can strongly enhance heat transfer; for example, radial ribs on the rotating disk increased stator heat transfer by up to 161.2% compared with a smooth reference case [32].

To improve such systems further, hybrid nanofluids are used because they suspend two or more different nanoparticles in a base fluid to obtain better thermophysical performance than many mono-nanofluids [33]. Recent review data indicate that hybrid nanofluids can provide up to 48% improvement in thermal conductivity and up to 123% enhancement in convective heat-transfer coefficient under suitable conditions [34]. For hybrid Casson nanofluids, suction/stretching are especially important because hybridization may enhance effective thermal conductivity while also increasing viscous resistance, so boundary-layer control is needed to balance heat removal against hydraulic penalty. When thermal radiation and thermal relaxation (Cattaneo–Christov heat flux) are incorporated, suction and stretching further govern the thermal diffusion pathway: radiation tends to elevate thermal-layer thickness, while thermal relaxation delays the heat-flux response and can suppress temperature diffusion. These same mechanisms are central to irreversibility because entropy generation depends on temperature gradients, viscous dissipation, and (in MHD problems) electromagnetic damping; hence, suction/stretching offer a practical route to manage entropy production and the Bejan-number split between heat-transfer and frictional irreversibility in rotating thermal systems [35–38]. Accordingly, adding hybrid nanoparticles to rotating thermal systems is attractive because the improved fluid properties can work together with rotation-induced mixing and boundary-layer modification to strengthen overall heat-transfer enhancement [27, 28, 34]. However, rotating systems and hybridization alone do not guarantee effective and reliable thermal performance, since stability issues of the dispersed and interacting phases must also be taken into account. One of the most important aspects of heat-transfer enhancement is the stability of the dispersed and interacting phases. For a Cu–Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O system, mutual thermodynamic and electrochemical stability is favored in neutral to mildly alkaline aqueous media, where copper is protected by a passive oxide film and magnetite remains comparatively stable without significant dissolution under near-neutral conditions [18, 39, 40]. For copper specifically, the protective behavior is closely linked to the formation and persistence of cuprous oxide (Cu\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O); recent studies show that Cu\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O is the main protective oxide over roughly neutral-to-alkaline conditions, while its solubility increases with increasing chloride concentration and temperature, which weakens passivity and accelerates corrosion [39, 41]. Furthermore, chloride-bearing and elevated-temperature environments are unfavorable because chloride promotes passive-film breakdown and localized attack on copper, while magnetite can also undergo electrolyte-dependent surface oxidation or phase transformation under aqueous electrochemical conditions [18, 39, 40].

While purely numerical solvers (e.g., collocation and shooting-based BVP solvers) are widely used for rotating-disk nanofluid models, semi-analytical approaches remain valuable because they can produce benchmark-quality series solutions, expose parameter sensitivities transparently, and provide convergence-controlled accuracy. HAM, in particular, offers explicit convergence control via auxiliary parameters and has been repeatedly employed in recent non-Newtonian/nanofluid contexts. For example, Alzahrani demonstrated close agreement between HAM and finite-difference solutions for a nanofluid transport model, emphasizing that HAM iterations can deliver high accuracy when convergence is properly controlled [42]. Likewise, recent entropy-optimized nanofluid studies have implemented HAM to analyze nonlinear coupled transport models with radiation and related effects [12, 26, 43, 44]. These methodological motivations are directly relevant to rotating-disk hybrid nanofluid problems, where simultaneous inclusion of MHD, porous resistance, suction, radiation, and non-Fourier heat flux yields strongly nonlinear coupled ODE systems.

Despite the expanding literature on Casson hybrid nanofluids and rotating-disk transport, a significant research gap exists in the development of a unified, multi-physics framework for water-based suspensions–specifically involving metal/metal-oxide combinations such as Cu–Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}–that simultaneously integrates magnetic forcing, porous resistance, suction, heat source/sink, radiation, and unsteadiness with non-Fourier thermal relaxation effects. Current models often treat these physical phenomena in isolation, leaving a lack of comprehensive data on second-law measures, such as entropy generation and the Bejan number, within a single integrated framework. Furthermore, there is a scarcity of controlled, side-by-side comparative evaluations of the velocity and temperature fields between Casson Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O and Casson (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O under identical rotating-disk conditions. Addressing these gaps requires not only a systematic analysis of these fields but also the establishment of reliable semi-analytical benchmarks via the Homotopy Analysis Method (HAM) to provide reproducible solutions for the strongly nonlinear, coupled similarity system.

Multi-physics integration: The study develops a unified unsteady rotating-disk Casson framework that simultaneously incorporates MHD, porous resistance, heat source/sink, thermal radiation, and non-Fourier thermal relaxation for both Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O and (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O suspensions, with a particular emphasis on suction as a vital boundary-layer control mechanism.

Comparative analysis: A direct, side-by-side comparative evaluation is conducted between the Casson Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O nanofluid and the Casson (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O hybrid nanofluid under identical governing conditions to clarify the relative thermal and hydrodynamic merits of mono versus hybrid coolants.

Comparative analysis: A direct, side-by-side comparative evaluation is conducted between the Casson Cu/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O nanofluid and the Casson (Cu+Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document})/H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O hybrid nanofluid under identical governing conditions to clarify the relative thermal and hydrodynamic merits of mono versus hybrid coolants.

Potential HAM implementation: The study leverages the potential of the Homotopy Analysis Method (HAM) to address the strongly coupled nonlinear similarity system, ensuring convergence control for complex multi-physical interactions.

Potential HAM implementation: The study leverages the potential of the Homotopy Analysis Method (HAM) to address the strongly coupled nonlinear similarity system, ensuring convergence control for complex multi-physical interactions.

Benchmark evaluation: The study performs a detailed evaluation of entropy generation and Bejan number, while utilizing HAM to establish a robust semi-analytical benchmark specifically for the skin friction coefficient and Nusselt number.

Mathematical problem formulation

Flow model

We investigate an incompressible magnetohydrodynamic flow of a viscously dissipative hybrid nanofluid composed of (Cu–Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document}) suspended in water base fluid above an unsteady stretchable rotating disk. The flow configuration is described in a non-rotating cylindrical coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(r,\theta ,z)$$\end{document}, where the velocity components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_r,u_\theta ,u_z)$$\end{document} are taken along the positive r-, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}-, and z-directions, respectively, as illustrated in Fig. 1.Fig. 1Schematic of porous rotating disk with MHD flow

Schematic of porous rotating disk with MHD flow

The disk is assumed to rotate about the z-axis with a time-dependent angular velocity [45, 46]\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_\omega = \frac{\omega r}{1-\gamma t}, $$\end{document}and to stretch in the radial direction with a time-dependent stretching velocity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_s = \frac{s r}{1-\gamma t}, $$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document} and s denote, respectively, the characteristic rotation and stretching rates of the disk, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ge 0$$\end{document} is a constant, t is the time variable, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma t < 1$$\end{document} ensures that the motion remains well-defined.

The hybrid nanofluid is further subjected to a time-dependent transverse magnetic field of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ B(t) = \frac{B_0}{(1-\gamma t)^{1/2}}, $$\end{document}acting normal to the disk surface, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_0$$\end{document} represents the uniform reference magnetic field strength. The temperature of the ambient fluid and that of the disk are prescribed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\infty $$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_w$$\end{document}, respectively. A uniform suction is imposed at the disk via\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_z(r,0,t) = w_0, \qquad w_0 = -\sqrt{\frac{2\omega \nu _f}{1 - \gamma t}} \, S, $$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S = f(0)$$\end{document} is the dimensionless suction parameter. In addition, the effects of viscous dissipation, as well as velocity and thermal slip at the disk surface, are taken into account. The Cattaneo–Christov heat flux model is adopted in order to capture non-Fourier heat conduction and to analyze the local heat transfer characteristics.

Under these assumptions, and by employing the Casson fluid model for the hybrid nanofluid, the continuity, momentum, and energy equations governing the unsteady flow over the time-dependent stretchable rotating disk can be written in the following form [47]:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\frac{\partial u_r}{\partial r} + \frac{u_r}{r} + \frac{\partial u_z}{\partial z} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} \rho _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial z}} - \frac{{u_{\theta }^{2} }}{r}} \right) = & \mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {1 + \frac{1}{\beta }} \right)\left( {\frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{r} }}{{\partial r^{2} }} + \frac{1}{r}\frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial r}} + \frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{r} }}{{\partial z^{2} }} - \frac{{u_{r} }}{{r^{2} }}} \right) \\ & \quad - \sigma _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} B(t)^{2} u_{r} - \frac{{\mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}{K}{\mkern 1mu} u_{r} . \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} \rho _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial z}} + \frac{{u_{r} u_{\theta } }}{r}} \right) = & \mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {1 + \frac{1}{\beta }} \right)\left( {\frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{\theta } }}{{\partial r^{2} }} + \frac{1}{r}\frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial r}} + \frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{\theta } }}{{\partial z^{2} }} - \frac{{u_{\theta } }}{{r^{2} }}} \right) \\ & \quad - \sigma _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} B(t)^{2} u_{\theta } - \frac{{\mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}{K}{\mkern 1mu} u_{\theta } . \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} \rho _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial z}}} \right) = & \mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {1 + \frac{1}{\beta }} \right)\left( {\frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{z} }}{{\partial r^{2} }} + \frac{1}{r}\frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial r}} + \frac{{\partial ^{2} u_{z} }}{{\partial z^{2} }}} \right) \\ & \quad - \frac{{\mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}{K}{\mkern 1mu} u_{z} . \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} (\rho c_{p} )_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {\frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial z}}} \right) = & - \nabla \cdot {\mathbf{q}} - \frac{{\partial q_{r} }}{{\partial z}} + \sigma _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} B_{0}^{2} (u_{r}^{2} + u_{\theta }^{2} ) \\ & \quad + \mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {1 + \frac{1}{\beta }} \right)\left[ {\left( {\frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial z}}} \right)^{2} + \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial z}}} \right)^{2} } \right] + Q_{0} (T - T_{\infty } ), \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}In the present model, the pressure is taken to be uniform, so that its spatial gradient vanishes. It is further assumed that the hybrid nanoparticles do not agglomerate within the hybrid nanofluid. Under this assumption, the hybrid nanoparticles and the base fluid are in local thermal equilibrium and there is no slip between them. Consequently, the heat flux vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{q}$$\end{document}, which obeys the Cattaneo-Christov heat flux model, can be written as6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tau _{t} \left( {\frac{{\partial {\mathbf{q}}}}{{\partial t}} + {\mathbf{u}} \cdot \nabla {\mathbf{q}} - {\mathbf{q}} \cdot \nabla {\mathbf{u}} + 4(\nabla \cdot {\mathbf{u}}){\mkern 1mu} {\mathbf{q}}} \right) + {\mathbf{q}} = - k{\mkern 1mu} \nabla T,{\text{ }} $$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{u}$$\end{document} is the velocity vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _t$$\end{document} is the relaxation time of the heat flux, and k denotes the thermal conductivity. For the present flow configuration, this expression reduces to7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _t\left( \frac{\partial \textbf{q}}{\partial t} + \textbf{u}\cdot \nabla \textbf{q} - \textbf{q}\cdot \nabla \textbf{u} \right) + \textbf{q} = -k\,\nabla T. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}By simplifying the above relation and substituting it for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\nabla \cdot \textbf{q}$$\end{document} in the energy equation, we obtain the modified energy balance. It is also apparent that the classical Fourier law is recovered in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _t = 0$$\end{document}, and Newtonian fluid model when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}. Furthermore, using the law of conservation of mass, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \cdot \textbf{u}=0$$\end{document} for viscous incompressible fluids, the energy equation for the present problem can be expressed in component form as8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} \frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial z}} = & \frac{{k_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}{{\left( {\rho c_{p} } \right)_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}\left( {\frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial r^{2} }} + \frac{1}{r}\frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial r}} + \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial z^{2} }}} \right) \\ & \quad - \tau _{t} \left[ {\frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial t^{2} }} + u_{r}^{2} \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial r^{2} }} + u_{z}^{2} \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial z^{2} }} + 2u_{r} \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial t{\mkern 1mu} \partial r}} + 2u_{z} \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial t{\mkern 1mu} \partial z}} + 2u_{r} u_{z} \frac{{\partial ^{2} T}}{{\partial r{\mkern 1mu} \partial z}}} \right] \\ & \quad - \tau _{t} \left[ {\left( {\frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial z}}} \right)\frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial r}} + \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial t}} + u_{r} \frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial r}} + u_{z} \frac{{\partial u_{z} }}{{\partial z}}} \right)\frac{{\partial T}}{{\partial z}}} \right] \\ & \quad + \frac{{\sigma _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} B^{2} }}{{(\rho c_{p} )_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}\left( {u_{r}^{2} + u_{\theta }^{2} } \right) + \frac{{\mu _{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} \left( {1 + \frac{1}{\beta }} \right)}}{{(\rho c_{p} )_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}\left[ {\left( {\frac{{\partial u_{r} }}{{\partial z}}} \right)^{2} + \left( {\frac{{\partial u_{\theta } }}{{\partial z}}} \right)^{2} } \right] \\ & \quad + \frac{{Q_{0} }}{{(\rho c_{p} )_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}\left( {T - T_{\infty } } \right) - \frac{1}{{(\rho c_{p} )_{{{\mathrm{hnf}}}} }}{\mkern 1mu} \frac{{\partial q_{r} }}{{\partial z}}. \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}where9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_r = -\frac{4\sigma ^{*}}{3k^{*}}\,\frac{\partial T^{4}}{\partial z} \approx -\frac{16\sigma ^{*}T_\infty ^{3}}{3k^{*}}\,\frac{\partial T}{\partial z}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}When the Eckert number is neglected, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ec=0$$\end{document}, the viscous dissipation effect disappears from the energy equation. Similarly, when the thermal relaxation parameter is set to zero, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _t=0$$\end{document}, the Cattaneo–Christov non-Fourier heat flux model reduces to the classical Fourier heat conduction model. Moreover, in the limiting case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}, the non-Newtonian Casson fluid model reduces to the classical Newtonian fluid model, since the Casson contribution becomes negligible.

The unsteady velocity and thermal slip boundary conditions are prescribed as follows. At the disk surface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=0$$\end{document}, the fluid satisfies the partial slip conditions for the radial and tangential velocities together with a thermal slip condition [45–47]:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_r&= u_s + L_0\!\left( 1 + \frac{1}{\beta }\right) \frac{\partial u_r}{\partial z}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\theta&= u_{\omega } + L_0\!\left( 1 + \frac{1}{\beta }\right) \frac{\partial u_\theta }{\partial z}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_z&= w_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T&= T_w + N_0\,\frac{\partial T}{\partial z} \quad \text {at } z = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Far from the surface, the fluid is quiescent and attains the ambient temperature:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_r \rightarrow 0,\qquad u_\theta \rightarrow 0,\qquad T \rightarrow T_\infty \quad \text {as } z \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The non-Newtonian Casson parameter is defined by15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta = \frac{\mu _c \sqrt{2\pi _c}}{P_y}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _c$$\end{document} denotes the Casson plastic dynamic viscosity, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_y$$\end{document} is the yield stress of the Casson fluid, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _c$$\end{document} is the critical value of the invariant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document} of the deformation-rate tensor.

The constitutive (rheological) relation for the Cauchy stress tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{ij}$$\end{document} of a Casson fluid can then be expressed in the form [43, 48, 49]16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \tau _{{ij}} = \left\{ {\begin{array}{*{20}c} {2\left( {\mu _{c} + \frac{{P_{y} }}{{\sqrt {2\pi _{c} } }}} \right)k_{{ij}} ,} & {\pi > \pi _{c} ,} \\ {2\mu _{c} k_{{ij}} + \frac{{P_{y} }}{{\sqrt {2\pi _{c} } }}{\mkern 1mu} k_{{ij}} ,} & {\pi < \pi _{c} ,} \\ \end{array} } \right. $$\end{document}where17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi = k_{ij}k_{ij} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}is the second invariant of the deformation-rate tensor, and18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k_{ij} = \frac{1}{2}\left( \frac{\partial u_i}{\partial x_j} + \frac{\partial u_j}{\partial x_i}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}is the rate-of-strain tensor. Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_j$$\end{document} are the components of the velocity vector.

Hybrid nanofluid model

The thermophysical properties used for H\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_2$$\end{document}O, Cu, and Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document} are consistent with recent nanofluid studies [50].

The thermophysical properties of the nanoparticles Cu [50–52] and Fe\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_3$$\end{document}O\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_4$$\end{document} [47, 48], together with those of the base fluid (water) [51, 53] at room temperature, are listed in Table 1 It is assumed that the base fluid and the suspended nanoparticles are in thermal equilibrium and that there is no slip between them. The effective thermophysical properties and models of the nanofluid and hybrid nanofluid are summarized in Tables 2, 3 respectively [45, 46].

Thermophysical properties of hybrid nanoparticles and their model

Similarity transformation and transformed equation

In order to reduce the governing partial differential equations to a more tractable form, it is convenient to transform them into a system of coupled nonlinear ordinary differential equations. Since obtaining analytical solutions of the original PDEs is rather difficult, we introduce the following similarity transformations, motivated by [45–48]:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\theta&= \frac{\omega r}{1-\gamma t}\, g(\eta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_z&= -\left( \frac{2\omega \nu _f}{1-\gamma t}\right) ^{1/2} f(\eta ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta (\eta )&= \frac{T - T_\infty }{T_w - T_\infty }, :28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&f(0)=S,\qquad f'(0)=S_r+\left( 1+\frac{1}{\beta }\right) \delta _v f''(0),\qquad g(0)=1+\left( 1+\frac{1}{\beta }\right) \delta _v g'(0), \nonumber \\&\theta (0)=1+\delta _t \theta '(0),\qquad f'(\eta )\rightarrow 0,\;\; g(\eta )\rightarrow 0,\;\; \theta (\eta )\rightarrow 0 \quad \text {as } \eta \rightarrow \infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\begin{aligned} \Lambda&= \frac{\gamma }{\omega } \qquad \text {(Dimensionless unsteadiness parameter)},\qquad M = \frac{\sigma _f (1-\gamma t) B_0^2}{\rho _f \omega } \qquad \text {(magnetic parameter)}, \\ \Pr&= \frac{\nu _f (\rho c_p)_f}{k_f} \qquad \text {(Prandtl number)},\qquad Ec = \frac{\omega ^2 r^2}{(c_p)_f (T_w - T_\infty )} \qquad \text {(Eckert number)}, \\ \alpha _t&= \frac{2\tau _t \omega }{1-\gamma t} \qquad \text {(thermal relaxation parameter)},\qquad Sr = \frac{s(1-\gamma t)}{\omega }{\nu _f(1-\gamma t)}} \qquad \text {(thermal slip parameter)},\\ \phi _1&= \frac{\rho _{hnf}}{\rho _f} =(1-\varphi _2)\left[ (1-\varphi _1)+\varphi _1\frac{\rho _{s_1}}{\rho _f}\right] +\varphi _2\frac{\rho _{s_2}}{\rho _f}, \\ \phi _2&= \frac{\mu _{hnf}}{\mu _f} =\frac{1}{(1-\varphi _1)^{2.5}(1-\varphi _2)^{2.5}}, \\ \phi _3&= \frac{\sigma _{hnf}}{\sigma _f} = \left( \frac{\sigma _{s_1}+2\sigma _f-2\varphi _1(\sigma _f-\sigma _{s_1})}{\sigma _{s_1}+2\sigma _f+\varphi _1(\sigma _f-\sigma _{s_1})} \right) \left( \frac{\sigma _{s_2}+2\sigma _{nf}-2\varphi _2(\sigma _{nf}-\sigma _{s_2})}{\sigma _{s_2}+2\sigma _{nf}+\varphi _2(\sigma _{nf}-\sigma _{s_2})} \right) , \\ \phi _4&= \frac{(\rho c_p)_{hnf}}{(\rho c_p)_f} =(1-\varphi _2)\left[ (1-\varphi _1)+\varphi _1\frac{(\rho c_p)_{s_1}}{(\rho c_p)_f}\right] +\varphi _2\frac{(\rho c_p)_{s_2}}{(\rho c_p)_f}, \\ \phi _5&= \frac{k_{hnf}}{k_f} = \left( \frac{k_{s_1}+2k_f-2\varphi _1(k_f-k_{s_1})}{k_{s_1}+2k_f+\varphi _1(k_f-k_{s_1})} \right) \left( \frac{k_{s_2}+2k_{nf}-2\varphi _2(k_{nf}-k_{s_2})}{k_{s_2}+2k_{nf}+\varphi _2(k_{nf}-k_{s_2})} \right) , \\&\varphi _1 \text { is the volumetric ratio of Cu},\qquad \varphi _2 \text { is the volumetric ratio of } \mathrm {Fe_3O_4}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Engineering curiosity

From an engineering point of view, the drag force and the local heat transfer rate at the disk surface can be quantified in terms of the skin–friction coefficient and the local Nusselt number, defined as [45, 46, 48]30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_f = \frac{\sqrt{\tau _{zr}^{2} + \tau _{z\theta }^{2}}}{\rho _f (\,\omega r\,)^{2}}, \qquad Nu_r = \frac{r\, q_w}{k_f (T_w - T_\infty )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{zr}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{z\theta }$$\end{document} are the radial and tangential shear stresses at the disk surface, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_w$$\end{document} is the wall heat flux.

For the present Casson hybrid nanofluid, the shear stresses at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=0$$\end{document} are given by31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _{zr} = -\mu _{\textrm{hnf}} \left( 1 + \frac{1}{\beta }\right) \left. \frac{\partial u_r}{\partial z}\right| _{z=0}, \qquad \tau _{z\theta } = -\mu _{\textrm{hnf}} \left( 1 + \frac{1}{\beta }\right) \left. \frac{\partial u_\theta }{\partial z}\right| _{z=0}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and the wall heat flux is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ q_w = -\left. \left( k_{\textrm{hnf}} + \frac{16\sigma ^{*}T_\infty ^{3}}{3k^{*}}\right) \,\frac{\partial T}{\partial z}\right| _{z=0} $$\end{document}Accordingly, the dimensionless skin–friction coefficient can be expressed in normalized form as32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{er}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_f=\sqrt{2}\phi _2\left( f''(0)^2+g'(0)^2\right) ^{\frac{1}{2}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re}_r$$\end{document} is the local Reynolds number.

The surface heat flux at the disk for the hybrid nanofluid is defined by33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_w = - k_{\textrm{hnf}} \left. \frac{\partial T}{\partial z}\right| _{z=0} = - k_{\textrm{hnf}} \left( T_w - T_\infty \right) \sqrt{\frac{2\omega }{\nu _f(1-\gamma t)}}\,\theta '(0), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}so that the dimensionless local heat–transfer rate (local Nusselt number) becomes34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{er}^{\frac{-1}{2}}Nu_f=-\sqrt{2}\left( \phi _5+N_r\right) \theta '(0) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{er}=\frac{ru_r}{\nu _f}=\frac{r^2\omega }{\nu _f(1-\gamma t)}$$\end{document} is the Reynolds number at the disk at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=0$$\end{document} Here the local Reynolds number based on the radial position r is given by35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Re}_r = \frac{\omega r^{2}}{\nu _f}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy}
